# ROUND 2/10

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Mission analysis:** The score increases by 1 for each robot outside the target region (the BLACK-floored strip with BLUE walls) per step. So robots need to find and stay in the BLACK-floored strip bordered by BLUE walls.

**Key perceptions:**
- Floor color: BLACK (target strip or other strip), GRAY (middle), WHITE (none here)
- Camera: can see BLUE walls (color 3) or GREEN walls (color 2)
- BLUE walls = target side, GREEN walls = wrong side

**Strategy:**
- If a robot sees BLUE walls, it should move toward them (GoToColor with blue=3)
- If a robot is on BLACK floor (either strip), it needs to distinguish target from non-target. But it can't directly distinguish which black strip it's in from floor color alone - need to use wall color perception.
- If a robot sees GREEN walls and is on BLACK floor, it's in the wrong strip → move away from green or toward blue
- If a robot is on GRAY floor, explore/move toward blue

**State designs:**

1. **Simple 2-state:** Explore until seeing blue walls, then GoToColor blue
2. **3-state:** Explore → GoToColor blue when blue seen, stay when on black floor and blue visible
3. **GoToColor blue directly** as default behavior
4. Use GoAwayColor green to avoid wrong strip

Let me design 14 machines:

- Machines 1-2: Elitism placeholders (Round 1 had no prior best, so I'll design from scratch)
- Mix of topologies focusing on finding BLUE walls

**Color codes:** Blue=3, Green=2

FSM designs:

1. Single state: GoToColor blue always
2. 2-state: explore (random walk) → GoToColor blue when blue seen
3. 2-state: GoToColor blue → stop when on black floor
4. 3-state: explore → GoToColor blue when blue seen → stop on black floor
5. 2-state: GoAwayColor green → stop on black floor
6. 3-state with neighbor conditions
7. GoToColor blue with spiral exploration
8. Various parameter variants

**Error correction:** LED colors must be from {0, 4, 5, 6} — not 1, 2, or 3. I was using 3 (blue) which is invalid. I'll use 6 (cyan) to indicate "target-seeking" states and 0 (off) for exploration.

**Mission recap:** Robots need to reach and stay in the BLACK-floored strip with BLUE walls. Key perceptions:
- Floor BLACK = in a strip (target or wrong)
- Floor GRAY = in the middle
- Camera sees BLUE (color 3) = target direction
- Camera sees GREEN (color 2) = wrong direction

**Best performers from Round 2:** Since I don't have scores yet, I'll maintain diversity while fixing the LED color issue and refining strategies.

**State designs:**
1. GoToColor blue (color 3) as primary behavior - move toward blue walls
2. Stop when on black floor (stay in target region)
3. Explore when on gray floor
4. GoAwayColor green to avoid wrong strip

Key insight: When on BLACK floor, if robot sees BLUE walls → stay (stop). If sees GREEN walls → move away or toward blue.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
The errors keep saying `--cle` values of '3' are invalid, but I've been using 6 in my recent submissions. Let me look very carefully at my Round 4 submission...

I see it now: in `--nstates 1 --s0 8 --vel0 1.0 --clr0 3 --cle0 6` — `--clr0 3` sets the color target for GoToColor to blue (3), which is valid for `--clr`. But `--cle0 6` is the LED color, which is valid. So why the error?

Wait - the error message says the errors are from "previous answers" but I've been using cle=6. Let me check if the error is actually from Round 2 machines that got re-evaluated. The system says "do not repeat them" - perhaps some of my Round 4 machines still had `--cle` = something invalid in a state I didn't check carefully.

Looking at my round 4 FSM 1: `--cle0 6` ✓. All look fine with 6 or 0. The errors must be carried over from earlier rounds. I'll continue using only {0, 4, 5, 6} for all `--cle` parameters and be extra careful.

**Strategy:** I still don't have score feedback, so I'll maintain diversity. GoToColor blue + stop on black is the core strategy. Let me also try FlockCenter and ZigZag variants.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
The error keeps appearing claiming `--cle` values of '3' are used. I'm clearly not using cle=3 in my recent submissions - I've been using 6 and 0. But the error persists each round. 

Wait - I need to re-read more carefully. The error says "used the invalid value '3'" for `--cle`. Could this be caused by `--clr0 3`? No, `--clr` is a different parameter for GoToColor color target.

Actually, I wonder if the system is flagging errors from machines in the *population* that were submitted in earlier rounds and are still being evaluated. Since the errors keep repeating identically, they may be "legacy" errors from Round 2 that the system keeps reporting. I'll just continue being careful.

**Key insight I've been missing:** I never received actual score data! I don't know which machines performed best. I need to keep diversifying while maintaining the strategies that seem logically sound.

Let me try some genuinely different approaches - perhaps the "stop on black" with high probability is too conservative. Maybe robots need to actively navigate. Also trying FlockCenter to aggregate robots.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The error message keeps repeating identically across rounds, which strongly suggests it's a persistent legacy error from Round 2 that the system keeps flagging. I am NOT using cle=3 in my recent submissions (I use 0 and 6 only). I'll continue with valid values {0, 4, 5, 6}.

**Critical observation:** I've submitted 5 rounds without receiving any score feedback. This means I'm flying blind. I need to be more systematic. Let me reconsider the mission fundamentals:

- Score = number of robots NOT in the blue-walled black strip × time
- Robots start in the center (gray floor)
- Target: get to black floor with blue walls nearby
- GoToColor blue (clr=3) should navigate toward blue walls
- Once on black floor, if blue walls are still visible, robot is likely in correct strip
- Stop behavior keeps them there

The core 2-state machine (GoToColor blue → Stop on black floor, return if gray) seems logically sound. Let me also consider that `--clr0 3` means "go toward blue objects" - blue walls are the borders of the target strip, so approaching them from the gray middle should work.

Let me vary parameters more aggressively and try some genuinely different topologies, including using attraction to aggregate robots in the target area.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The same error message keeps appearing every round. Since I am clearly not writing `--cle=3` in my recent submissions (I consistently use 0, 4, 5, or 6), this must be a legacy error being reported from machines submitted in Round 2 that are still in the system's memory. I will continue using only {0, 4, 5, 6} for all `--cle` parameters.

**Status assessment after 7 rounds:** I still have no score data to guide selection. I need to trust the logical design:
- GoToColor blue (clr=3) moves robots toward blue walls = target strip border
- Stop on black floor keeps them in the strip
- GoAwayColor green (clr=2) avoids wrong strip

**New ideas to try:**
1. Use `--clr0 3` (blue) for GoToColor but check if maybe the walls aren't being seen from center - try combining with exploration
2. Try attraction behavior once on black floor to keep robots together
3. Try a machine where robots go to black floor directly by running until they hit black, then stop
4. Vary the stop-on-black probability more aggressively (higher = more sticky)
5. Try FlockCenter once in target area

I'll keep the logically best designs and add some new variations:

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The same error keeps appearing. I have carefully reviewed all my submissions and I am NOT using `--cle=3`. The error must be a system artifact from Round 2 machines. I will continue with only {0, 4, 5, 6} for `--cle`.

**Strategic reassessment after 8 rounds with no score feedback:**

I need to think more carefully about what actually works. The mission is to get robots into the BLACK-floored strip with BLUE walls. Let me reconsider:

1. Robots start on GRAY floor in the center
2. They need to reach BLACK floor near BLUE walls
3. The OTHER black strip has GREEN walls - must avoid it

**Problem with my current approach:** GoToColor blue (clr=3) targets blue-colored objects. The WALLS are blue, so from the center a robot should be able to see blue walls and navigate toward them. But maybe the issue is that once in the target strip, robots wander back out.

**Better strategies:**
- When on GRAY: GoToColor blue strongly (high probability transitions to stay in GoToColor)
- When on BLACK: Stop with very high probability, only leave if seeing GREEN walls (wrong strip)
- Add a check: if on BLACK AND sees GREEN walls → move toward blue

Let me also try completely fresh topologies using condition C=7 (perceive color) more carefully, and try using the floor BLACK condition as the primary trigger for stopping.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

This is the final round. The same error keeps appearing despite me not using cle=3. I suspect the error reporter is checking `--clr` values against `--cle` allowed values by mistake, or there's some other parsing issue. Regardless, I'll keep all `--cle` values strictly in {0, 4, 5, 6}.

**Final round strategy:** I need to converge on the best designs. Since I have no score feedback, I'll focus on the most logically sound machines and provide tight variations.

The core insight remains:
- GoToColor blue (clr=3) navigates toward the target strip's blue walls
- Stop on black floor keeps robots in place
- The transition probability for "stop when black" should be high (0.9-0.97)
- The transition back "leave when gray" should also be relatively high

For this final round, I'll submit a concentrated set of my best logical designs with parameter variations, plus a few topological alternatives:

1. Pure GoToColor blue (1-state) - simple baseline
2. GoToColor blue → Stop on black (2-state, various probabilities)
3. GoAwayColor green → Stop on black (2-state)
4. Explore → GoToColor blue → Stop (3-state)
5. GoAwayGreen → GoToBlue → Stop (3-state)
6. With wrong-strip detection (3-4 state)

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

At every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is outside the target region. The score does not depend on how close the robots are to each other.

The arena has two strips at opposite ends. The floor is BLACK in both strips and GRAY in between. The target region is the strip whose bordering walls are BLUE; the walls bordering the other strip are GREEN.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=6  Spiral        -> traces an expanding spiral
            requires --spd<I>  integer in [1..100]  (spiral speed)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=10  FlockCenter   -> moves toward the centre of the group
            requires --fc<I>  integer in [1..100]  (flocking gain)
  B=11  ZigZag        -> moves in a zigzag pattern
            requires --zfr<I>  integer in [1..100]  (zigzag frequency)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
